manifestation: Afterward I feel awful about myself, like I’ve wasted food I meant to share and I’m too old to be acting this way, and I usually sit there ashamed and promise it won’t happen again.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating occurs, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: Looking back at my kitchen calendar and food bank weeks, these episodes have been happening most weeks, often once or twice, for many months now.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I still show up for my volunteer shifts and craft fairs, but I’ve started avoiding some shared meals afterward because I’m embarrassed and don’t want people asking why I seem upset.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and two months, ever since my close friend from the food bank died.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 48 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S050\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these episodes when you're alone, or do you also do it when others are around?\n\nPatient: Always alone. Yes, always alone. I... I don't think I could ever do this in front of someone else. I mean, just the thought of my husband walking in while I'm standing there at the counter, shoveling food into my mouth like a wild animal... it makes my stomach turn. \n\nI make very sure that I'm by myself. Like I was saying, my husband works full time, so he's gone during the day. And since I'm not working right now, I have the house to myself for hours at a time. It usually happens in the mid-afternoon, around 2:00 or 3:00, when it's just so quiet and there's no chance of anyone dropping by. I'll even check out the front window sometimes, just to make sure the mailman isn't walking up to the porch or a neighbor isn't out in their yard where they could see me through the kitchen window. \n\nIf we have people over, or if we're at a family gathering or something, I eat completely normally. Probably less than normal, honestly, because I'm so hyper-aware of what I'm putting on my plate. I'll just have a small portion of salad and maybe a little piece of chicken, and I'll eat it very slowly, using my knife and fork properly, dabbing my mouth with a napkin... playing the part of the polite, put-together woman who has total control over her appetite. \n\nBut then, the second I'm back home, and the door is locked, and my husband goes out to the garage to tinker with his tools or whatever... that's when it hits me. It's like this switch flips because I know I'm finally unobserved. I don't think I've ever binged with another human being in the room, or even in the house if I thought there was a chance they might walk into the kitchen. The shame of someone seeing me like that... seeing the sheer volume of food I'm eating, and how fast I'm eating it, and the glazed-over look I probably have in my eyes... I think I would just die of embarrassment right on the spot. So yes, it's always, always when I'm completely alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset, anxious, or stressed right before one of these episodes of eating happens?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because... well, actually, no. Not really. I know that’s what people usually think of, right? You see it on TV or read about it in magazines—the whole \"emotional eating\" thing, where someone has a terrible day at work, or they get into a huge fight with their husband, and then they just dive headfirst into a pint of ice cream while crying on the couch. But for me, it’s honestly not like that at all. \n\nI could be having a perfectly fine day. A really nice day, even! Like, just last week, it was a Tuesday, I think. I had a lovely morning. I went out and watered my hydrangeas—they're doing so well this year, by the way—and I sat on the patio with a cup of coffee and read a few chapters of my book. I wasn’t stressed about money, my husband and I are getting along just fine, I don't have a demanding boss yelling at me since I'm not working... there was absolutely nothing to be anxious about. My life is actually very peaceful right now. \n\nBut then... it just happens anyway. It’s almost like it creeps up on me when things are totally calm, or maybe just when things are a little *too* quiet, you know? Sometimes I actually wish I *was* upset or stressed out right before it happens, because then it would at least make some sense! I could point to it and say, 'Oh, well, I was really worried about this or that, so I ate to cope with the anxiety.' But I don't have that excuse. I'm not coping with any big crisis. I'll just be feeling completely neutral, or even in a good mood, and then suddenly that urge hits me out of absolutely nowhere. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I feel upset or anxious beforehand. It’s just this strange, unprovoked thing that takes over when everything in my day is going perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any specific emotions right before you start eating in these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really don't think I feel much of anything. I mean, not any specific emotion, anyway. It's not sadness, it's not anger, it's not joy or celebration. It's just... I don't know, a blankness? Or maybe just an urge that isn't connected to a feeling at all. \n\nI know people always say we eat our feelings, but I really feel like I'm not feeling anything at all when it starts. Like, just yesterday, for instance, I was folding laundry in the bedroom. Towels, mostly. My husband likes those really big, heavy bath sheets, and they take up so much room in the linen closet, so I have to fold them a very specific way, in thirds, just to make them fit. I was just standing there at the foot of the bed, matching up the corners, feeling perfectly content. Maybe a little bored if I absolutely had to pick an emotion, but not really bored, just... existing, you know? Going about my day. \n\nAnd then, bam. The thought of the pantry just pops into my head. It wasn't preceded by a feeling of, 'oh, I'm so lonely' or 'oh, I'm so frustrated with these heavy towels.' It's just this physical drive, almost. Like a magnet pulling me down the hall and into the kitchen. So if you're looking for a specific emotion, like grief or anger or even just regular old sadness... I really can't give you one. It's just this sudden, overwhelming need to eat, completely disconnected from whatever mood I was in two seconds prior. \n\nIt's incredibly frustrating, honestly, because if I could just pinpoint a feeling, maybe I could work on it! I could say, 'Oh, I'm feeling lonely, I should call my sister.' But since there's no emotion there to begin with, I don't even know what I'm supposed to be fixing. It's just... nothing.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually feel uncomfortably full after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Uncomfortably full? Hmm... you know, I really haven't noticed that. I don't think I do. I mean, I know that's probably what you'd expect, right? Because of how much I'm eating. You hear people talk about eating until their stomach hurts, like that feeling on Thanksgiving where you just have to unbutton your jeans and lie down on the sofa. My husband gets like that sometimes if we go out to eat—there's this little Italian place we love, Mario's, and he'll get the huge portion of chicken parmesan and just eat himself silly until he's groaning about how full he is and rubbing his belly. 

But for me, during these times at home... it's not like that at all. I don't really register my stomach feeling stretched or painful. It's actually kind of strange now that I'm saying it out loud, because you'd think I would! I know I've eaten a massive amount of food because I can literally see the evidence right in front of me on the counter—like a whole sleeve of Ritz crackers or half a loaf of bread just gone in a matter of minutes—but physically, in my belly? I don't feel like I'm bursting. 

I really don't feel much of anything physically. It's more like a total numbness. I just kind of snap out of it when a certain package of food runs out, or when I just suddenly wake up from that weird trance I get into, but I never really get to that point where I'm doubled over in pain or feeling physically sick from being too full. I just don't seem to have that physical sensation of being stuffed, even though logically I know my stomach has to be full. It's just... I don't feel it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thank you. Well, I... I guess I'm just really struggling lately, and it’s getting to a point where I just don’t know what to do with myself anymore. Since I'm not working right now, I spend a lot of time at home during the day, you know, just doing things around the house, running errands, and I inevitably end up spending a lot of time near the kitchen. And what really brings me in today, what honestly just terrifies me, is what happens when I'm in there by myself. \n\nIt’s like... well, once I start eating, it’s not just having a normal snack like a normal person. It’s like this switch flips. Especially when I’m completely alone in the house, just standing there at the kitchen counter. What scares me is that it literally feels like I’ve just stepped aside, almost like I'm watching someone else take over my body, and I absolutely cannot make myself stop. The whole time, my brain is going, and I'm literally telling myself, ‘Janet, put it away. Just close the cabinet, put the box down, walk away,’ but my hands just keep going. It's so incredibly scary because I know I shouldn't be doing it, and I can hear my own voice in my head pleading with me to stop, but I just... I can't. I have absolutely zero control in those moments, and it's just been weighing on me so heavily that I knew I had to come in and talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating when you feel like you've lost control?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, it's... it's just the worst feeling in the world. Honestly, the moment it's over, the moment I finally stop and look around at the wrappers or the empty boxes, this wave of just... absolute disgust washes over me. I feel awful about myself. Just terrible. I sit there and look at what I've done, and I think about how some of that food was supposed to be for dinner, or snacks for when my husband gets home, and I’ve just ruined it. I’ve wasted it all. And then the guilt really sets in, you know? Because I'm 48 years old. I'm a grown woman. I have an associate's degree, I've lived a whole life, and I'm sitting on the kitchen floor or standing at the counter acting like a child who got into the cookie jar, except it's so much worse because I know better. \n\nI just sit there, sometimes for a long time, just wallowing in how ashamed I am. I feel so pathetic. And I always, every single time, I tell myself, 'Janet, this is the last time. You are never, ever doing this again.' I make these grand promises to myself that I'm going to get it together, that tomorrow will be different, that I won't buy those foods anymore, or I won't go into the kitchen when I'm alone... but then, of course, the cycle just repeats itself. But in that moment afterward, the shame is just so heavy, I can barely breathe. It's like I'm carrying this huge, embarrassing secret that I can't explain to anyone, not even myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating an excessive amount of food during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Yes, absolutely. It's... it's definitely an excessive amount, and I think that's part of what makes the shame so bad afterward. Because it's not just, you know, having an extra cookie or a second helping of dinner. It's so much more than that. \n\nLike, for example, I volunteer sometimes at the local food bank, or sometimes I'll bake things for the senior center events—I like to stay busy, you know, since I'm not working—and on some of those afternoons, I'll come home, and it's like the floodgates just open. I'll walk in the door, and before I even really take my coat off properly, I'm in the kitchen. I'll start with something that seems normal, like a full plate of leftovers from the fridge, maybe a big portion of a casserole or whatever we had the night before. But then, it doesn't stop there. I'll finish that, and then I'll cut myself a slice of banana bread. And then another slice. And then I'll move on to something salty, so I'll open a bag of chips and eat, I don't know, half the bag. Then I'll slice up some cheese, just standing there eating it off the block. And then I'll remember the cookie tin we keep on the counter, and I'll just eat whatever cookies are left in there until they're gone. \n\nIt's just this constant, frantic grazing, moving from one thing to the next, sweet to salty to sweet again. And it's only later, when I'm looking at all the empty packaging and the crumbs everywhere, that I really process how much I just consumed. I'll look at it all and realize, my goodness, that was way, way more food than I would ever put on a plate to serve to another person. It's more than a huge meal, all just crammed into this short window of time. So, yes, it's definitely an excessive amount. It's embarrassing to even list it all out like that, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, exactly. And I know I touched on this a little bit already, but it really is the most terrifying part of the whole thing. It’s exactly that—I just cannot stop. It’s like I mentioned before, when I’m standing there in the kitchen, completely by myself, it honestly feels like I’ve just... stepped aside. Like my body has been hijacked by somebody else, or like I'm on some horrible autopilot. \n\nI’ll be standing there, maybe eating the chips or the cookies, and the rational part of my brain is fully awake. I’m literally screaming at myself in my head. I’m thinking, ‘Janet, put it away. Just close the bag. Put the box down and walk into the living room.’ I know exactly what I’m doing, and I know I don’t want to be doing it, but my hands just keep moving. They just keep reaching into the bag and bringing the food to my mouth. It’s like there’s a total disconnect between my brain and my body. I can’t force myself to stop, no matter how hard I plead with myself. It’s just this complete and utter loss of control, and it doesn't end until I'm either physically in pain from being so full, or until the food is just... gone. It's so scary to feel like you can't even control your own hands, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes, oh my gosh, yes. I know I went on about this a bit right at the beginning, but the guilt is... it's just all-consuming. It really is. Like I said before, afterward, I just feel completely awful about myself. It's this heavy, sinking feeling right in the pit of my stomach that just won't go away. \n\nI'll just sit there, maybe on the kitchen stool or even just leaning against the counter, and I look at the absolute mess I've made, and all I can think about is how I’ve wasted all this food. Food that I meant to share! Like a toddler who can't be trusted alone in the pantry, except it's so much worse, because I'm an adult and I know better. And I always, always sit there in the aftermath of it all and promise myself that it won’t ever happen again. I swear up and down to myself that I'm done, that tomorrow is a fresh start, that I'll throw out the junk food and never buy it again... but that guilt, it just hangs over me like a dark cloud for days afterward, no matter how many promises I make.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. Yes. It's actually funny you ask that because... well, not funny ha-ha, but just... it's strange how that works. I'll catch myself eating just so incredibly fast during all of this. \n\nLike, I'll be standing there at the kitchen counter, and I'm just taking bite after bite, barely even chewing sometimes, I think. And the really sad part is, I barely even taste the food. I could have spent an hour making a really nice casserole for dinner, or maybe I spent the morning baking those muffins I mentioned earlier for the senior center, and I'll just be shoveling them in. You'd think if I went to all the trouble to bake something from scratch, to measure out the ingredients and get the house smelling so nice, I'd want to sit down at the table with a cup of tea and actually enjoy the flavor, right? But no, it's not like that at all. It's just... bite, swallow, reach for the next one, bite, swallow. \n\nIt's like I'm in this frantic hurry. Like I'm running late for a train or something! Which makes absolutely no sense, because, like I said, I'm not working right now. My afternoons are usually wide open. I'll just be standing there in my own kitchen, my husband is at work for another three hours, the house is quiet... there is absolutely nowhere I need to be and nothing pressing I need to do. I have all the time in the world. But I'm just cramming the food in like someone is about to come into the room and snatch it right out of my hands. \n\nI don't know why I rush like that. Maybe it's because deep down I know I shouldn't be doing it, so my brain is trying to just get it over with as quickly as possible? Or maybe it's just part of that autopilot feeling I was talking about earlier, where the engine is just stuck on high speed. It's all just a blur. I'll look down and a whole row of muffins is gone in what feels like two minutes, and I couldn't even tell you if they turned out moist or dry because I didn't take the time to actually taste a single crumb of them. It’s just rush, rush, rush, for absolutely no reason at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these episodes when you're alone, or do you also do it when others are around?\n\nPatient: Always alone. I'll even check out the front window sometimes, just to make sure the mailman isn't walking up to the porch or a neighbor isn't out in their yard where they could see me through the kitchen window. \n\nIf we have people over, or if we're at a family gathering or something, I eat completely normally. So yes, it's always, always when I'm completely alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset, anxious, or stressed right before one of these episodes of eating happens?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because... well, actually, no. Not really. I know that’s what people usually think of, right? You see it on TV or read about it in magazines—the whole \"emotional eating\" thing, where someone has a terrible day at work, or they get into a huge fight with their husband, and then they just dive headfirst into a pint of ice cream while crying on the couch. But for me, it’s honestly not like that at all. \n\nI could be having a perfectly fine day. A really nice day, even! Like, just last week, it was a Tuesday, I think. I had a lovely morning. I went out and watered my hydrangeas—they're doing so well this year, by the way—and I sat on the patio with a cup of coffee and read a few chapters of my book. I wasn’t stressed about money, my husband and I are getting along just fine, I don't have a demanding boss yelling at me since I'm not working... there was absolutely nothing to be anxious about. My life is actually very peaceful right now. \n\nBut then... it just happens anyway. It’s almost like it creeps up on me when things are totally calm, or maybe just when things are a little *too* quiet, you know? Sometimes I actually wish I *was* upset or stressed out right before it happens, because then it would at least make some sense! I could point to it and say, 'Oh, well, I was really worried about this or that, so I ate to cope with the anxiety.' But I don't have that excuse. I'm not coping with any big crisis. I'll just be feeling completely neutral, or even in a good mood, and then suddenly that urge hits me out of absolutely nowhere. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I feel upset or anxious beforehand. It’s just this strange, unprovoked thing that takes over when everything in my day is going perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any specific emotions right before you start eating in these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really don't think I feel much of anything. I mean, not any specific emotion, anyway. It's not sadness, it's not anger, it's not joy or celebration. It's just... I don't know, a blankness? Or maybe just an urge that isn't connected to a feeling at all. \n\nI know people always say we eat our feelings, but I really feel like I'm not feeling anything at all when it starts. Like, just yesterday, for instance, I was folding laundry in the bedroom. Towels, mostly. My husband likes those really big, heavy bath sheets, and they take up so much room in the linen closet, so I have to fold them a very specific way, in thirds, just to make them fit. I was just standing there at the foot of the bed, matching up the corners, feeling perfectly content. Maybe a little bored if I absolutely had to pick an emotion, but not really bored, just... existing, you know? Going about my day. \n\nAnd then, bam. The thought of the pantry just pops into my head. It wasn't preceded by a feeling of, 'oh, I'm so lonely' or 'oh, I'm so frustrated with these heavy towels.' It's just this physical drive, almost. Like a magnet pulling me down the hall and into the kitchen. So if you're looking for a specific emotion, like grief or anger or even just regular old sadness... I really can't give you one. It's just this sudden, overwhelming need to eat, completely disconnected from whatever mood I was in two seconds prior. \n\nIt's incredibly frustrating, honestly, because if I could just pinpoint a feeling, maybe I could work on it! I could say, 'Oh, I'm feeling lonely, I should call my sister.' But since there's no emotion there to begin with, I don't even know what I'm supposed to be fixing. It's just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Uncomfortably full? Hmm... you know, I really haven't noticed that. I don't think I do. I mean, I know that's probably what you'd expect, right? Because of how much I'm eating. You hear people talk about eating until their stomach hurts, like that feeling on Thanksgiving where you just have to unbutton your jeans and lie down on the sofa. My husband gets like that sometimes if we go out to eat—there's this little Italian place we love, Mario's, and he'll get the huge portion of chicken parmesan and just eat himself silly until he's groaning about how full he is and rubbing his belly. \n\nBut for me, during these times at home... it's not like that at all. I don't really register my stomach feeling stretched or painful. It's actually kind of strange now that I'm saying it out loud, because you'd think I would! I know I've eaten a massive amount of food because I can literally see the evidence right in front of me on the counter—like a whole sleeve of Ritz crackers or half a loaf of bread just gone in a matter of minutes—but physically, in my belly? I don't feel like I'm bursting. \n\nI really don't feel much of anything physically. It's more like a total numbness. I just kind of snap out of it when a certain package of food runs out, or when I just suddenly wake up from that weird trance I get into, but I never really get to that point where I'm doubled over in pain or feeling physically sick from being too full. I just don't seem to have that physical sensation of being stuffed, even though logically I know my stomach has to be full. It's just... I don't feel it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Weight changes? You know, that’s another thing that’s just so strange about all of this. I really haven't noticed any significant changes. I mean, I step on the scale in my bathroom—it's one of those digital ones, I got it at Target a few years ago because the old dial one was getting so hard to read without my glasses—and the number is pretty much exactly what it's been for the last, I don't know, five or six years? 

I know that probably sounds impossible. I mean, my sister, for example, she just looks at a piece of cake and gains three pounds. She's always trying some new diet, cutting out carbs or only eating between certain hours of the day, and her weight just yo-yos constantly up and down, up and down. She's always complaining about having to buy different sizes of jeans depending on the season. But me? I've been wearing the exact same size 10 pants for as long as I can remember. 

It honestly baffles me sometimes. When I think about the sheer volume of food I'm going through during one of these... whatever you want to call them... these eating spells... you'd think I'd be putting on ten pounds a month! Like I was saying before about the crackers and the bread, it's just so many calories all at once. And it's not like I'm running marathons to burn it off, either. I do my gardening, and I walk the dog—he's a golden retriever mix, very sweet, but he's getting older so our walks are more like slow strolls around the block these days. 

But no, my clothes fit exactly the same. My wedding ring still slides on and off just fine, my face hasn't gotten any rounder. I don't feel heavy or sluggish the next day like I've gained weight. It's almost like my body just completely ignores all that extra food. Or maybe my metabolism is just really good at hiding it? I don't know. But if you were to look at my medical records from my primary care doctor, Dr. Evans, you'd see my weight has been incredibly stable. So, no, I haven't noticed any significant changes at all, despite everything.

I know I've eaten a massive amount of food because I can literally see the evidence right in front of me on the counter—like a whole sleeve of Ritz crackers or half a loaf of bread just gone in a matter of minutes—but physically, in my belly? I don't feel like I'm bursting. \n\nI really don't feel much of anything physically. It's more like a total numbness. I just kind of snap out of it when a certain package of food runs out, or when I just suddenly wake up from that weird trance I get into, but I never really get to that point where I'm doubled over in pain or feeling physically sick from being too full. I just don't seem to have that physical sensation of being stuffed, even though logically I know my stomach has to be full. It's just... I don't feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Weight changes? You know, that’s another thing that’s just so strange about all of this. I really haven't noticed any significant changes. I mean, I step on the scale in my bathroom—it's one of those digital ones, I got it at Target a few years ago because the old dial one was getting so hard to read without my glasses—and the number is pretty much exactly what it's been for the last, I don't know, five or six years? \n\nI know that probably sounds impossible. I mean, my sister, for example, she just looks at a piece of cake and gains three pounds. She's always trying some new diet, cutting out carbs or only eating between certain hours of the day, and her weight just yo-yos constantly up and down, up and down. She's always complaining about having to buy different sizes of jeans depending on the season. But me? I've been wearing the exact same size 10 pants for as long as I can remember. \n\nIt honestly baffles me sometimes. When I think about the sheer volume of food I'm going through during one of these... whatever you want to call them... these eating spells... you'd think I'd be putting on ten pounds a month! Like I was saying before about the crackers and the bread, it's just so many calories all at once. And it's not like I'm running marathons to burn it off, either. I do my gardening, and I walk the dog—he's a golden retriever mix, very sweet, but he's getting older so our walks are more like slow strolls around the block these days. \n\nBut no, my clothes fit exactly the same. My wedding ring still slides on and off just fine, my face hasn't gotten any rounder. I don't feel heavy or sluggish the next day like I've gained weight. It's almost like my body just completely ignores all that extra food. Or maybe my metabolism is just really good at hiding it? I don't know. But if you were to look at my medical records from my primary care doctor, Dr. Evans, you'd see my weight has been incredibly stable. So, no, I haven't noticed any significant changes at all, despite everything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I'll just be feeling completely neutral, or even in a good mood, and then suddenly that urge hits me out of absolutely nowhere. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I feel upset or anxious beforehand. It’s just this strange, unprovoked thing that takes over when everything in my day is going perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any specific emotions right before you start eating in these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really don't think I feel much of anything. I could say, 'Oh, I'm feeling lonely, I should call my sister.' But since there's no emotion there to begin with, I don't even know what I'm supposed to be fixing. It's just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Uncomfortably full? Hmm... you know, I really haven't noticed that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, gosh, yes. Yes, I do. That’s... honestly, that’s the worst part of all of this. Once it's over, once I finally snap out of that trance I was telling you about, the reality of what I've just done comes crashing down on me, and I just feel completely awful about myself. 

I'll look at the empty wrappers or the empty boxes on the counter, and my mind just starts going a mile a minute with all these terrible thoughts. Like, just last week, I had bought these special bakery cookies—the really nice ones with the little dollop of raspberry jam in the middle—because my neighbor, Susan, usually comes over for coffee on Tuesday mornings. And I ate all of them. Every single one. And afterward, I just sat there staring at the plastic container, feeling so incredibly selfish. I just kept thinking about how I’ve wasted this beautiful food that I bought specifically to share with a friend. It makes me feel like such a terrible hostess, and just a bad person in general, honestly.

And then I start in on my age. I mean, I'm forty-eight years old. I'm a grown woman. I've managed a household for decades, I've got my associate's degree, I know how to be a responsible adult in literally every other area of my life. And I just think to myself, I am way too old to be acting this way. It feels like something a rebellious teenager would do, sneaking food in the kitchen, not a woman approaching fifty! 

So I usually just sit there at the kitchen island, completely swallowed up by this horrible, heavy shame. It's just so embarrassing, even though no one else is in the room to see it. I sit there feeling ashamed, and I always, always promise myself that it won’t happen again. I swear up and down, 'That's it, this was the last time, tomorrow is a fresh start.' I make these grand promises to myself right there on the kitchen stool. But the things I say to myself in my own head while I'm sitting there... they're just relentless. I really, really beat myself up over it.

I can’t force myself to stop, no matter how hard I plead with myself. You hear people talk about eating until their stomach hurts, like that feeling on Thanksgiving where you just have to unbutton your jeans and lie down on the sofa. My husband gets like that sometimes if we go out to eat—there's this little Italian place we love, Mario's, and he'll get the huge portion of chicken parmesan and just eat himself silly until he's groaning about how full he is and rubbing his belly. \n\nBut for me, during these times at home... it's not like that at all. I don't really register my stomach feeling stretched or painful. It's actually kind of strange now that I'm saying it out loud, because you'd think I would! I know I've eaten a massive amount of food because I can literally see the evidence right in front of me on the counter—like a whole sleeve of Ritz crackers or half a loaf of bread just gone in a matter of minutes—but physically, in my belly? I don't feel like I'm bursting. \n\nI really don't feel much of anything physically. It's more like a total numbness. I just kind of snap out of it when a certain package of food runs out, or when I just suddenly wake up from that weird trance I get into, but I never really get to that point where I'm doubled over in pain or feeling physically sick from being too full. I just don't seem to have that physical sensation of being stuffed, even though logically I know my stomach has to be full. It's just... I don't feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Weight changes? You know, that’s another thing that’s just so strange about all of this. I really haven't noticed any significant changes. I mean, I step on the scale in my bathroom—it's one of those digital ones, I got it at Target a few years ago because the old dial one was getting so hard to read without my glasses—and the number is pretty much exactly what it's been for the last, I don't know, five or six years? \n\nI know that probably sounds impossible. I mean, my sister, for example, she just looks at a piece of cake and gains three pounds. She's always trying some new diet, cutting out carbs or only eating between certain hours of the day, and her weight just yo-yos constantly up and down, up and down. She's always complaining about having to buy different sizes of jeans depending on the season. But me? I've been wearing the exact same size 10 pants for as long as I can remember. \n\nIt honestly baffles me sometimes. When I think about the sheer volume of food I'm going through during one of these... whatever you want to call them... these eating spells... you'd think I'd be putting on ten pounds a month! Like I was saying before about the crackers and the bread, it's just so many calories all at once. And it's not like I'm running marathons to burn it off, either. I do my gardening, and I walk the dog—he's a golden retriever mix, very sweet, but he's getting older so our walks are more like slow strolls around the block these days. \n\nBut no, my clothes fit exactly the same. My wedding ring still slides on and off just fine, my face hasn't gotten any rounder. I don't feel heavy or sluggish the next day like I've gained weight. It's almost like my body just completely ignores all that extra food. Or maybe my metabolism is just really good at hiding it? I don't know. But if you were to look at my medical records from my primary care doctor, Dr. Evans, you'd see my weight has been incredibly stable. So, no, I haven't noticed any significant changes at all, despite everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, yes. Yes, I do. That’s... honestly, that’s the worst part of all of this. Once it's over, once I finally snap out of that trance I was telling you about, the reality of what I've just done comes crashing down on me, and I just feel completely awful about myself. \n\nI'll look at the empty wrappers or the empty boxes on the counter, and my mind just starts going a mile a minute with all these terrible thoughts. Like, just last week, I had bought these special bakery cookies—the really nice ones with the little dollop of raspberry jam in the middle—because my neighbor, Susan, usually comes over for coffee on Tuesday mornings. And I ate all of them. Every single one. And afterward, I just sat there staring at the plastic container, feeling so incredibly selfish. I just kept thinking about how I’ve wasted this beautiful food that I bought specifically to share with a friend. It makes me feel like such a terrible hostess, and just a bad person in general, honestly.\n\nAnd then I start in on my age. I mean, I'm forty-eight years old. I'm a grown woman. I've managed a household for decades, I've got my associate's degree, I know how to be a responsible adult in literally every other area of my life. And I just think to myself, I am way too old to be acting this way. It feels like something a rebellious teenager would do, sneaking food in the kitchen, not a woman approaching fifty! \n\nSo I usually just sit there at the kitchen island, completely swallowed up by this horrible, heavy shame. It's just so embarrassing, even though no one else is in the room to see it. I sit there feeling ashamed, and I always, always promise myself that it won’t happen again. I swear up and down, 'That's it, this was the last time, tomorrow is a fresh start.' I make these grand promises to myself right there on the kitchen stool. But the things I say to myself in my own head while I'm sitting there... they're just relentless. I really, really beat myself up over it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. calendar and food bank weeks, these episodes have been happening most weeks, often once or twice, for many months now.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I still show up for my volunteer shifts and craft fairs, but I’ve started avoiding some shared meals afterward because I’m embarrassed and don’t want people asking why I seem upset.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and two months, ever since my close friend from the food bank died.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 48 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S050\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Uncomfortably full? Hmm... you know, I really haven't noticed that. I don't think I do. I mean, I know that's probably what you'd expect, right? Because of how much I'm eating. You hear people talk about eating until their stomach hurts, like that feeling on Thanksgiving where you just have to unbutton your jeans and lie down on the sofa. My husband gets like that sometimes if we go out to eat—there's this little Italian place we love, Mario's, and he'll get the huge portion of chicken parmesan and just eat himself silly until he's groaning about how full he is and rubbing his belly. \n\nBut for me, during these times at home... it's not like that at all. I don't really register my stomach feeling stretched or painful. It's actually kind of strange now that I'm saying it out loud, because you'd think I would! I know I've eaten a massive amount of food because I can literally see the evidence right in front of me on the counter—like a whole sleeve of Ritz crackers or half a loaf of bread just gone in a matter of minutes—but physically, in my belly? I mean, my sister, for example, she just looks at a piece of cake and gains three pounds. She's always complaining about having to buy different sizes of jeans depending on the season. But me? When I think about the sheer volume of food I'm going through during one of these... whatever you want to call them... these eating spells... you'd think I'd be putting on ten pounds a month! I do my gardening, and I walk the dog—he's a golden retriever mix, very sweet, but he's getting older so our walks are more like slow strolls around the block these days. \n\nBut no, my clothes fit exactly the same. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes... exactly like I was just saying a minute ago. It really does make me feel like a genuinely bad person. It all just ties right back into that awful, heavy feeling I get about myself. 

Like I mentioned with those raspberry cookies for Susan—it's not just about the food itself, it's the fact that I took something meant for someone else, something meant to be shared and enjoyed together, and I just completely wasted it. When you do things like that, how can you not feel like a bad person, you know? It feels almost deceitful. I'll be sitting there on that same wooden stool at the kitchen island—the one with the squeaky leg my husband keeps meaning to fix—and I just dwell on my age again. I'm forty-eight. I should know better! I should be so far past this kind of behavior, but instead, I'm acting like someone who has absolutely no moral compass or self-control. 

It's just so deeply shameful. I sit there in the quiet house, usually just staring at the grain of the wood on the counter, feeling absolutely terrible about who I am. And that's when I make those promises to myself all over again. I tell myself, 'I am going to be better, I am going to be a good person tomorrow, this is never, ever going to happen again.' I really mean it in that moment, too. But the fact that I keep having to sit there, feeling so ashamed, making those exact same promises... it just reinforces how awful I feel about myself in the first place.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thank you. Well, I... I guess I'm just really struggling lately, and it’s getting to a point where I just don’t know what to do with myself anymore. Since I'm not working right now, I spend a lot of time at home during the day, you know, just doing things around the house, running errands, and I inevitably end up spending a lot of time near the kitchen. And what really brings me in today, what honestly just terrifies me, is what happens when I'm in there by myself. \n\nIt’s like... well, once I start eating, it’s not just having a normal snack like a normal person. It’s like this switch flips. Especially when I’m completely alone in the house, just standing there at the kitchen counter. The whole time, my brain is going, and I'm literally telling myself, ‘Janet, put it away. Just close the cabinet, put the box down, walk away,’ but my hands just keep going. It's so incredibly scary because I know I shouldn't be doing it, and I can hear my own voice in my head pleading with me to stop, but I just... I can't. I have absolutely zero control in those moments, and it's just been weighing on me so heavily that I knew I had to come in and talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating when you feel like you've lost control?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, it's... it's just the worst feeling in the world. Honestly, the moment it's over, the moment I finally stop and look around at the wrappers or the empty boxes, this wave of just... absolute disgust washes over me. I feel awful about myself. Just terrible. I sit there and look at what I've done, and I think about how some of that food was supposed to be for dinner, or snacks for when my husband gets home, and I’ve just ruined it. I’ve wasted it all. And then the guilt really sets in, you know? Because I'm 48 years old. I'm a grown woman. I have an associate's degree, I've lived a whole life, and I'm sitting on the kitchen floor or standing at the counter acting like a child who got into the cookie jar, except it's so much worse because I know better. \n\nI just sit there, sometimes for a long time, just wallowing in how ashamed I am. I feel so pathetic. And I always, every single time, I tell myself, 'Janet, this is the last time. You are never, ever doing this again.' I make these grand promises to myself that I'm going to get it together, that tomorrow will be different, that I won't buy those foods anymore, or I won't go into the kitchen when I'm alone... but then, of course, the cycle just repeats itself. But in that moment afterward, the shame is just so heavy, I can barely breathe. It's like I'm carrying this huge, embarrassing secret that I can't explain to anyone, not even myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating an excessive amount of food during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Yes, absolutely. It's... it's definitely an excessive amount, and I think that's part of what makes the shame so bad afterward. Because it's not just, you know, having an extra cookie or a second helping of dinner. It's so much more than that. \n\nLike, for example, I volunteer sometimes at the local food bank, or sometimes I'll bake things for the senior center events—I like to stay busy, you know, since I'm not working—and on some of those afternoons, I'll come home, and it's like the floodgates just open. I'll walk in the door, and before I even really take my coat off properly, I'm in the kitchen. I'll start with something that seems normal, like a full plate of leftovers from the fridge, maybe a big portion of a casserole or whatever we had the night before. But then, it doesn't stop there. I'll finish that, and then I'll cut myself a slice of banana bread. And then another slice. And then I'll move on to something salty, so I'll open a bag of chips and eat, I don't know, half the bag. Then I'll slice up some cheese, just standing there eating it off the block. And then I'll remember the cookie tin we keep on the counter, and I'll just eat whatever cookies are left in there until they're gone. \n\nIt's just this constant, frantic grazing, moving from one thing to the next, sweet to salty to sweet again. And it's only later, when I'm looking at all the empty packaging and the crumbs everywhere, that I really process how much I just consumed. I'll look at it all and realize, my goodness, that was way, way more food than I would ever put on a plate to serve to another person. It's more than a huge meal, all just crammed into this short window of time. So, yes, it's definitely an excessive amount. It's embarrassing to even list it all out like that, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, exactly. And I know I touched on this a little bit already, but it really is the most terrifying part of the whole thing. It’s exactly that—I just cannot stop. It’s like I mentioned before, when I’m standing there in the kitchen, completely by myself, it honestly feels like I’ve just... stepped aside. Like my body has been hijacked by somebody else, or like I'm on some horrible autopilot. \n\nI’ll be standing there, maybe eating the chips or the cookies, and the rational part of my brain is fully awake. I’m literally screaming at myself in my head. I’m thinking, ‘Janet, put it away. Just close the bag. Put the box down and walk into the living room.’ I know exactly what I’m doing, and I know I don’t want to be doing it, but my hands just keep moving. They just keep reaching into the bag and bringing the food to my mouth. It’s like there’s a total disconnect between my brain and my body. I can’t force myself to stop, no matter how hard I plead with myself. It’s just this complete and utter loss of control, and it doesn't end until I'm either physically in pain from being so full, or until the food is just... gone. It's so scary to feel like you can't even control your own hands, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes, oh my gosh, yes. I know I went on about this a bit right at the beginning, but the guilt is... it's just all-consuming. It really is. Like I said before, afterward, I just feel completely awful about myself. It's this heavy, sinking feeling right in the pit of my stomach that just won't go away. \n\nI'll just sit there, maybe on the kitchen stool or even just leaning against the counter, and I look at the absolute mess I've made, and all I can think about is how I’ve wasted all this food. Food that I meant to share! Like, I'll see the empty wrappers of the snacks I specifically bought for my husband's lunches, or the remnants of something I was planning to serve for dinner, and I realize I just ruined it. I ate it all, and for what? And I just sit there in the quiet kitchen, completely ashamed, thinking about how I'm 48 years old. I'm a grown woman. I have an associate's degree, I've lived a whole life... I am way, way too old to be acting this way. \n\nIt makes me feel so foolish, honestly. Like a toddler who can't be trusted alone in the pantry, except it's so much worse, because I'm an adult and I know better. And I always, always sit there in the aftermath of it all and promise myself that it won’t ever happen again. I swear up and down to myself that I'm done, that tomorrow is a fresh start, that I'll throw out the junk food and never buy it again... but that guilt, it just hangs over me like a dark cloud for days afterward, no matter how many promises I make.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. Yes. It's actually funny you ask that because... well, not funny ha-ha, but just... it's strange how that works. I'll catch myself eating just so incredibly fast during all of this. \n\nLike, I'll be standing there at the kitchen counter, and I'm just taking bite after bite, barely even chewing sometimes, I think. And the really sad part is, I barely even taste the food. I could have spent an hour making a really nice casserole for dinner, or maybe I spent the morning baking those muffins I mentioned earlier for the senior center, and I'll just be shoveling them in. You'd think if I went to all the trouble to bake something from scratch, to measure out the ingredients and get the house smelling so nice, I'd want to sit down at the table with a cup of tea and actually enjoy the flavor, right? But no, it's not like that at all. It's just... bite, swallow, reach for the next one, bite, swallow. \n\nIt's like I'm in this frantic hurry. Like I'm running late for a train or something! Which makes absolutely no sense, because, like I said, I'm not working right now. My afternoons are usually wide open. I'll just be standing there in my own kitchen, my husband is at work for another three hours, the house is quiet... there is absolutely nowhere I need to be and nothing pressing I need to do. I have all the time in the world. But I'm just cramming the food in like someone is about to come into the room and snatch it right out of my hands. \n\nI don't know why I rush like that. Maybe it's because deep down I know I shouldn't be doing it, so my brain is trying to just get it over with as quickly as possible? Or maybe it's just part of that autopilot feeling I was talking about earlier, where the engine is just stuck on high speed. It's all just a blur. I'll look down and a whole row of muffins is gone in what feels like two minutes, and I couldn't even tell you if they turned out moist or dry because I didn't take the time to actually taste a single crumb of them. It’s just rush, rush, rush, for absolutely no reason at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these episodes when you're alone, or do you also do it when others are around?\n\nPatient: Always alone. Yes, always alone. I... I don't think I could ever do this in front of someone else. I mean, just the thought of my husband walking in while I'm standing there at the counter, shoveling food into my mouth like a wild animal... it makes my stomach turn. \n\nI make very sure that I'm by myself. Like I was saying, my husband works full time, so he's gone during the day. And since I'm not working right now, I have the house to myself for hours at a time. It usually happens in the mid-afternoon, around 2:00 or 3:00, when it's just so quiet and there's no chance of anyone dropping by. I'll even check out the front window sometimes, just to make sure the mailman isn't walking up to the porch or a neighbor isn't out in their yard where they could see me through the kitchen window. \n\nIf we have people over, or if we're at a family gathering or something, I eat completely normally. Probably less than normal, honestly, because I'm so hyper-aware of what I'm putting on my plate. I'll just have a small portion of salad and maybe a little piece of chicken, and I'll eat it very slowly, using my knife and fork properly, dabbing my mouth with a napkin... playing the part of the polite, put-together woman who has total control over her appetite. \n\nBut then, the second I'm back home, and the door is locked, and my husband goes out to the garage to tinker with his tools or whatever... that's when it hits me. It's like this switch flips because I know I'm finally unobserved. I don't think I've ever binged with another human being in the room, or even in the house if I thought there was a chance they might walk into the kitchen. The shame of someone seeing me like that... seeing the sheer volume of food I'm eating, and how fast I'm eating it, and the glazed-over look I probably have in my eyes... I think I would just die of embarrassment right on the spot. So yes, it's always, always when I'm completely alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset, anxious, or stressed right before one of these episodes of eating happens?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because... well, actually, no. Not really. I know that’s what people usually think of, right? You see it on TV or read about it in magazines—the whole \"emotional eating\" thing, where someone has a terrible day at work, or they get into a huge fight with their husband, and then they just dive headfirst into a pint of ice cream while crying on the couch. But for me, it’s honestly not like that at all. \n\nI could be having a perfectly fine day. A really nice day, even! Like, just last week, it was a Tuesday, I think. I had a lovely morning. I went out and watered my hydrangeas—they're doing so well this year, by the way—and I sat on the patio with a cup of coffee and read a few chapters of my book. I wasn’t stressed about money, my husband and I are getting along just fine, I don't have a demanding boss yelling at me since I'm not working... there was absolutely nothing to be anxious about. My life is actually very peaceful right now. \n\nBut then... it just happens anyway. It’s almost like it creeps up on me when things are totally calm, or maybe just when things are a little *too* quiet, you know? Sometimes I actually wish I *was* upset or stressed out right before it happens, because then it would at least make some sense! I could point to it and say, 'Oh, well, I was really worried about this or that, so I ate to cope with the anxiety.' But I don't have that excuse. I'm not coping with any big crisis. I'll just be feeling completely neutral, or even in a good mood, and then suddenly that urge hits me out of absolutely nowhere. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I feel upset or anxious beforehand. It’s just this strange, unprovoked thing that takes over when everything in my day is going perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any specific emotions right before you start eating in these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really don't think I feel much of anything. I mean, not any specific emotion, anyway. It's not sadness, it's not anger, it's not joy or celebration. It's just... I don't know, a blankness? Or maybe just an urge that isn't connected to a feeling at all. \n\nI know people always say we eat our feelings, but I really feel like I'm not feeling anything at all when it starts. Like, just yesterday, for instance, I was folding laundry in the bedroom. Towels, mostly. My husband likes those really big, heavy bath sheets, and they take up so much room in the linen closet, so I have to fold them a very specific way, in thirds, just to make them fit. I was just standing there at the foot of the bed, matching up the corners, feeling perfectly content. Maybe a little bored if I absolutely had to pick an emotion, but not really bored, just... existing, you know? Going about my day. \n\nAnd then, bam. The thought of the pantry just pops into my head. It wasn't preceded by a feeling of, 'oh, I'm so lonely' or 'oh, I'm so frustrated with these heavy towels.' It's just this physical drive, almost. Like a magnet pulling me down the hall and into the kitchen. So if you're looking for a specific emotion, like grief or anger or even just regular old sadness... I really can't give you one. It's just this sudden, overwhelming need to eat, completely disconnected from whatever mood I was in two seconds prior. \n\nIt's incredibly frustrating, honestly, because if I could just pinpoint a feeling, maybe I could work on it! I could say, 'Oh, I'm feeling lonely, I should call my sister.' But since there's no emotion there to begin with, I don't even know what I'm supposed to be fixing. It's just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Uncomfortably full? Hmm... you know, I really haven't noticed that. I don't think I do. I mean, I know that's probably what you'd expect, right? Because of how much I'm eating. You hear people talk about eating until their stomach hurts, like that feeling on Thanksgiving where you just have to unbutton your jeans and lie down on the sofa. My husband gets like that sometimes if we go out to eat—there's this little Italian place we love, Mario's, and he'll get the huge portion of chicken parmesan and just eat himself silly until he's groaning about how full he is and rubbing his belly. \n\nBut for me, during these times at home... it's not like that at all. I don't really register my stomach feeling stretched or painful. It's actually kind of strange now that I'm saying it out loud, because you'd think I would! I know I've eaten a massive amount of food because I can literally see the evidence right in front of me on the counter—like a whole sleeve of Ritz crackers or half a loaf of bread just gone in a matter of minutes—but physically, in my belly? I don't feel like I'm bursting. \n\nI really don't feel much of anything physically. It's more like a total numbness. I just kind of snap out of it when a certain package of food runs out, or when I just suddenly wake up from that weird trance I get into, but I never really get to that point where I'm doubled over in pain or feeling physically sick from being too full. I just don't seem to have that physical sensation of being stuffed, even though logically I know my stomach has to be full. It's just... I don't feel it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Weight changes? You know, that’s another thing that’s just so strange about all of this. I really haven't noticed any significant changes. I mean, I step on the scale in my bathroom—it's one of those digital ones, I got it at Target a few years ago because the old dial one was getting so hard to read without my glasses—and the number is pretty much exactly what it's been for the last, I don't know, five or six years? \n\nI know that probably sounds impossible. I mean, my sister, for example, she just looks at a piece of cake and gains three pounds. She's always trying some new diet, cutting out carbs or only eating between certain hours of the day, and her weight just yo-yos constantly up and down, up and down. She's always complaining about having to buy different sizes of jeans depending on the season. But me? I've been wearing the exact same size 10 pants for as long as I can remember. \n\nIt honestly baffles me sometimes. When I think about the sheer volume of food I'm going through during one of these... whatever you want to call them... these eating spells... you'd think I'd be putting on ten pounds a month! Like I was saying before about the crackers and the bread, it's just so many calories all at once. And it's not like I'm running marathons to burn it off, either. I do my gardening, and I walk the dog—he's a golden retriever mix, very sweet, but he's getting older so our walks are more like slow strolls around the block these days. \n\nBut no, my clothes fit exactly the same. My wedding ring still slides on and off just fine, my face hasn't gotten any rounder. I don't feel heavy or sluggish the next day like I've gained weight. It's almost like my body just completely ignores all that extra food. Or maybe my metabolism is just really good at hiding it? I don't know. But if you were to look at my medical records from my primary care doctor, Dr. Evans, you'd see my weight has been incredibly stable. So, no, I haven't noticed any significant changes at all, despite everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, yes. Yes, I do. That’s... honestly, that’s the worst part of all of this. Once it's over, once I finally snap out of that trance I was telling you about, the reality of what I've just done comes crashing down on me, and I just feel completely awful about myself. \n\nI'll look at the empty wrappers or the empty boxes on the counter, and my mind just starts going a mile a minute with all these terrible thoughts. Like, just last week, I had bought these special bakery cookies—the really nice ones with the little dollop of raspberry jam in the middle—because my neighbor, Susan, usually comes over for coffee on Tuesday mornings. And I ate all of them. Every single one. And afterward, I just sat there staring at the plastic container, feeling so incredibly selfish. I just kept thinking about how I’ve wasted this beautiful food that I bought specifically to share with a friend. It makes me feel like such a terrible hostess, and just a bad person in general, honestly.\n\nAnd then I start in on my age. I mean, I'm forty-eight years old. I'm a grown woman. I've managed a household for decades, I've got my associate's degree, I know how to be a responsible adult in literally every other area of my life. And I just think to myself, I am way too old to be acting this way. It feels like something a rebellious teenager would do, sneaking food in the kitchen, not a woman approaching fifty! \n\nSo I usually just sit there at the kitchen island, completely swallowed up by this horrible, heavy shame. It's just so embarrassing, even though no one else is in the room to see it. I sit there feeling ashamed, and I always, always promise myself that it won’t happen again. I swear up and down, 'That's it, this was the last time, tomorrow is a fresh start.' I make these grand promises to myself right there on the kitchen stool. But the things I say to myself in my own head while I'm sitting there... they're just relentless. I really, really beat myself up over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a bad person after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... exactly like I was just saying a minute ago. It really does make me feel like a genuinely bad person. It all just ties right back into that awful, heavy feeling I get about myself. \n\nLike I mentioned with those raspberry cookies for Susan—it's not just about the food itself, it's the fact that I took something meant for someone else, something meant to be shared and enjoyed together, and I just completely wasted it. When you do things like that, how can you not feel like a bad person, you know? It feels almost deceitful. I'll be sitting there on that same wooden stool at the kitchen island—the one with the squeaky leg my husband keeps meaning to fix—and I just dwell on my age again. I'm forty-eight. I should know better! I should be so far past this kind of behavior, but instead, I'm acting like someone who has absolutely no moral compass or self-control. \n\nIt's just so deeply shameful. I sit there in the quiet house, usually just staring at the grain of the wood on the counter, feeling absolutely terrible about who I am. And that's when I make those promises to myself all over again. I tell myself, 'I am going to be better, I am going to be a good person tomorrow, this is never, ever going to happen again.' I really mean it in that moment, too. But the fact that I keep having to sit there, feeling so ashamed, making those exact same promises... it just reinforces how awful I feel about myself in the first place.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My wedding ring still slides on and off just fine, my face hasn't gotten any rounder. I don't feel heavy or sluggish the next day like I've gained weight. It's almost like my body just completely ignores all that extra food. Or maybe my metabolism is just really good at hiding it? I don't know. But if you were to look at my medical records from my primary care doctor, Dr. Evans, you'd see my weight has been incredibly stable. So, no, I haven't noticed any significant changes at all, despite everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, yes. Yes, I do. That’s... honestly, that’s the worst part of all of this. Once it's over, once I finally snap out of that trance I was telling you about, the reality of what I've just done comes crashing down on me, and I just feel completely awful about myself. \n\nI'll look at the empty wrappers or the empty boxes on the counter, and my mind just starts going a mile a minute with all these terrible thoughts. Like, just last week, I had bought these special bakery cookies—the really nice ones with the little dollop of raspberry jam in the middle—because my neighbor, Susan, usually comes over for coffee on Tuesday mornings. And I ate all of them. Every single one. And afterward, I just sat there staring at the plastic container, feeling so incredibly selfish. I just kept thinking about how I’ve wasted this beautiful food that I bought specifically to share with a friend. It makes me feel like such a terrible hostess, and just a bad person in general, honestly.\n\nAnd then I start in on my age. I mean, I'm forty-eight years old. I'm a grown woman. I've managed a household for decades, I've got my associate's degree, I know how to be a responsible adult in literally every other area of my life. And I just think to myself, I am way too old to be acting this way. It feels like something a rebellious teenager would do, sneaking food in the kitchen, not a woman approaching fifty! \n\nSo I usually just sit there at the kitchen island, completely swallowed up by this horrible, heavy shame. It's just so embarrassing, even though no one else is in the room to see it. I sit there feeling ashamed, and I always, always promise myself that it won’t happen again. I swear up and down, 'That's it, this was the last time, tomorrow is a fresh start.' I make these grand promises to myself right there on the kitchen stool. But the things I say to myself in my own head while I'm sitting there... they're just relentless. I really, really beat myself up over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a bad person after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... exactly like I was just saying a minute ago. It really does make me feel like a genuinely bad person. It all just ties right back into that awful, heavy feeling I get about myself. \n\nLike I mentioned with those raspberry cookies for Susan—it's not just about the food itself, it's the fact that I took something meant for someone else, something meant to be shared and enjoyed together, and I just completely wasted it. I should know better! I should be so far past this kind of behavior, but instead, I'm acting like someone who has absolutely no moral compass or self-control. \n\nIt's just so deeply shameful. I sit there in the quiet house, usually just staring at the grain of the wood on the counter, feeling absolutely terrible about who I am. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
You know, I've really tried to figure that out. I sit there and try to piece it together like a puzzle, but honestly... no, I don't think so. I really don't. 

It's not like I only do this when I'm upset or stressed out. I mean, my life is actually pretty calm right now. My husband works a steady job, the kids are grown and out of the house, so it's just the two of us and the dog. I don't have the stress of a commute or a boss breathing down my neck since I don't work outside the home anymore. And sure, I get anxious sometimes—who doesn't? Like when I'm waiting for the plumber to show up and he's three hours late, or when I'm trying to figure out the new property tax forms. But those eating spells? They happen even on perfectly good days. 

Like last Tuesday, the day before the cookie incident... it was a beautiful day. I had spent the whole morning out in the garden, pulling weeds and planting some new petunias in the front beds. The sun was shining, I was listening to a really good audiobook—one of those historical mysteries I love—and I felt completely relaxed. Happy, even. And then, I came inside to wash my hands and get a glass of iced tea, and before I knew it, I was standing at the pantry door, tearing into a box of graham crackers and half a loaf of sourdough. 

There was no sadness, no anxiety, no argument with my husband... nothing. It just happened. It's almost like a switch flips in my brain, completely independent of how I'm feeling emotionally. I've read articles in magazines, you know, at the dentist's office, about "emotional eating," and how people eat a pint of ice cream when they're crying over a breakup. But that's just not me. I could be having the most wonderful, peaceful day, and it still happens. So, as much as I've tried to find a connection to stress or being upset, I just can't say that's what triggers it. It really seems to come out of nowhere, regardless of my mood.
